This is the defeaturing Ansys Mechanical (its defeature size; MESH-9 cites its factor) and Abaqus/CAE (Geometry Edit, remove small fillets) apply before a stiffness or far-field stress mesh. Its face keeps its shape the way a micro round does (the elements' curved sides follow it), and the wall rule still holds there. Holes and notches are never cosmetic, nor is a fillet with no wall reading. The plan names every one and says in plain words that the stress right at them is not resolved and reads lower than the true peak, and to ask for them to be refined if a peak there matters (fatigue, a crack starting there). Shared dev, 1 Nov: a 640 mm part's 29 fillets of 0.1 mm in 14-77 mm walls asked for 0.026 mm elements under a 64 mm base - see Corpus planning sweep (lab, 1 Nov: 308 parts and assemblies planned on main and on the branch): it fires on 1 of them (asm_ox_cryo_interface, an r 0.2 mm fillet: 18.3M to 15.6M elements); the 640 mm part's plan loses its 0.026 mm zones.. |
| PLAN-fea-25 | in force | **A micro round's own edge break is no wall** (`engines/fea_plan.round_walls`, rulebook 49). The wall ray from a round far smaller than its part (a PLAN-fea-12 micro round, or a cosmetic fillet) crosses its own edge break and meets the neighbouring face within about two radii; a reading of at most 3 radii there is that gap, not a wall, so it sets no wall size and no thin spot, and a sharp corner beside such a round is capped by the wall of its other faces (PLAN-fea-5). A real thin wall is never dropped: the flat faces either side of any real thin material read it themselves and keep their 2 elements through it (PLAN-fea-3), and a round reading more than 3 radii keeps its wall. Shared dev, 1 Nov: a 0.05 mm edge break read a '0.104 mm wall' and asked for 0.052 mm elements. Corpus proof: On the 308-plan sweep: 2 wall zones and 2 thin spots were read as edge breaks, every one on a round under 1/1,000 of its part reading at most 3 radii; no other wall or thin spot of any plan changed (0 real walls lost).. |
| PLAN-fea-26 | in force | **A plan that will be slow says so before it meshes** (`engines/fea_plan.slow_ratio`, rulebook 49). When the base size is more than 300 times the finest zone, the plan's words say the sizes run from the base down to that zone (named, with the ratio) and that the surface stage will be slow on every face such small zones touch; the why record keeps the ratio. A warning only: nothing is changed by it. Calibrated on 240 corpus parts planned on main beside their corpus run (lab, 1 Nov): under 100 to 1 the median part meshed at 0.5 ms an element (221 parts), 100-300 at 2.2 ms (16), over 300 at 15-25 ms, and those 3 failed. |
| PLAN-fea-27 | in force | **An element count the user named scales the WHOLE plan** (rulebook 57: no largest factor stops it any more - past the accuracy minimums it goes on to PLAN-fea-30, the words say asked, planned and built, and a smaller count never plans more) (Areen, 5 Nov; `engines/fea_driver.target_plan`, `engines/fea_plan.scaled`). The count is the AI's reading of the notes (AI-fea-4). The rules' own plan is built and estimated first (N0); then ONE factor s multiplies every size - the base and every zone (holes, fillets, notches, walls, thin spots, rounds, capped corners, the parts of an assembly) - so the fine areas stay finer than the rest in the same proportion. s is solved on the real estimate (the size field's histogram), not on a formula: a first guess from count ~ 1/size^3, then the line through the nearest estimate on each side of the count (log count against log s), within 5% of the count in at most 6 estimates past the rules' own. A RANGE is aimed at its middle and any estimate inside it is a hit: the solve stops as soon as one lands there (the rules' own plan inside it is kept), and the words report against the range. It works both ways: under N0 coarser, over it finer - up to the most the largest machine allowed holds (PLAN-fea-8's limit, aimed at 0.85 of it with the solve's 5% to spare), never past it. **The hard minimums are re-applied after scaling**, and where one holds a zone the rest takes up the slack (the solve follows the real estimate, so the other sizes go further): 2 elements through every wall (PLAN-fea-3; a hot spot's and a corner's wall too), 4 per quarter-turn of every curved face (PLAN-fea-7; a hole, fillet or notch is one), a capped sharp corner never finer than its cap (PLAN-fea-5), growth 1.3 (PLAN-fea-6), and the set-asides as ever (PLAN-fea-10, PLAN-fea-12: the micro details are judged against the scaled plan's finest size). A hot spot made coarser than PLAN-fea-4's accuracy size (6 per quarter-turn, 3 through the wall beside it) is named, with "the peak stress there is less accurate", and the mesh check holds it to what its scaled size gives, so the ladder does not tighten it back (LAD-fea-3). When the minimums stop the count above what was asked, the words say the least count they allow and the minimum that sets it ("a count under about 766k would break 2 elements through the 0.0448 mm wall under Face_250"). "At most" a count the rules' plan already meets, or "at least" one it already passes, keeps the rules' plan. Every later pass keeps the factor. A scaled plan still over the largest machine goes on to PLAN-fea-8's coarsening, said. MEASURED (5 Nov): the textbook groove (Kt 2.193, 3%): the rules' 8,681 elements +1.29%; asked for a third (2,900; s = 1.52, built 4,094) +2.05%; asked for three times (26,000; s = 0.60, built 27,461) +0.92%. The 446-face LPBF hinge: the rules plan 1.18M; asked for 1M, s = 1.15, planned 975k (4 estimates); asked for 3M, s = 0.64, planned 3.04M (2 estimates); asked for a third (393k), s = 2.08 and the minimums stop it at 766k (854 walls at 2 through, 89 curves at 4 per quarter-turn). Planning costs one size-field histogram per estimate (the hinge: 17 s each). |
| PLAN-fea-28 | in force | **Under the user's count the base grows to a fifth of the part at most** (`engines/fea_plan.TARGET_MIN_DIVISIONS`) - twice PLAN-fea-14's tenth. The base is the size only where no rule asks for finer: every wall keeps 2 elements through it and every curved face 4 per quarter-turn whatever the base, so the base sets only the bulk of a chunky part. At a fifth of the diagonal a cube still has about 3 quadratic elements along every edge; past it, the walls' own 2-through (half the cube) would decide, and a part would be one or two elements across. Measured (5 Nov): a deep cantilever (L/h = 3, where the base sets the bulk) at the rules' base -0.17% from Timoshenko's stiffness, its base 1.46 times coarser -0.10%; the groove asked for 1,000 elements was the same mesh (4,094) with the base allowed to a fifth of the part or to two-fifths - its curves' and walls' minimums decide there, not the base. An assembly's parts' own bases (PLAN-fea-17) are held the same way, each at a fifth of its part. |
| PLAN-fea-29 | in force | **The sizes the user asked for per part win** (Areen's cofounder, 5 Nov: the user is the engineer; our rules advise, they do not overrule an explicit request; `engines/fea_plan.user_sized`). The sizes come from the notes through AI-fea-9 as {part: [min, max]} (a single number is min = max; a single part is Part_1). In such a part every size - its own base, held all through the part as PLAN-fea-17's part base is (the middle of the range), and every zone in it: walls, thin spots, curves, hot spots, corners, the AI's refinements - is held inside [min, max]: a rule asking finer than min stops at min. Where that leaves a wall under 2 elements through it, a curve under 4 per quarter-turn, a hot spot under its stress size or a corner over its cap, the plan carries on and the words name it with what it costs ("bending stress in those walls is less accurate"); the mesh check is held to what the size gives (`wall_elements`, `per_quarter`) and the ladder never tightens a plan of the user's choice (LAD-fea-3, `engines/fea_ladder.by_choice`). Only PLAN-fea-30's validity limits stop a user size, said with the size it got. The plan's base is the coarsest part's middle at least, and where a sized part meets another the sizes blend over a short distance (said). With an element count as well, the sizes come first and the count moves only the parts not sized (said). A plan of the user's choice that no pass builds valid is never a failed run: the rules' own plan is meshed instead, said in the words and the result (`engines/fea_driver.drive`). MEASURED (5 Nov, lab): a 9-part bonded assembly (a 533 mm hollow beam with bolts, washers, bosses and brackets) asked for 3-4 mm on the beam and 1.5-2 mm on the rest: the rules plan about 1.9M elements; with the sizes, planned 165k, built 172k, valid on the first pass (worst SICN 0.11), its 1.22 mm skin-side walls at about 0.4 elements through and its 0.45 mm bolt fillets held at 0.24 mm (3 per quarter-turn), each said. |
| PLAN-fea-30 | in force | **Only validity stops what the user asked for** (`engines/fea_plan.valid_cap`, `engines/fea_driver.target_plan`). A size per part (PLAN-fea-29) or an element count (PLAN-fea-27) goes past the accuracy minimums (2 through every wall, 4 per quarter-turn of every curve, the corner cap, a hot spot's 6 and 3) when it must, and stops only where elements would not build valid: every curved face keeps 3 elements per quarter-turn (`VALID_PER_QUARTER`) and every part 3 elements across its diagonal (`VALID_DIVISIONS`). A wall is no validity limit. MEASURED (lab ~/feabudget_lab/valid, 5 Nov, gmsh as the adapter runs it, quadratic): flat and bent plates 0.5-2 mm thick built valid at every size tried up to 16 times their wall (worst scaled Jacobian 0.17-1.0); 1-2 mm tubes of 10-11.5 mm bore failed ("a segment and a facet intersect") at 1.96 and 1.44 elements per quarter-turn and built at 2.16 and more. A count is met in order: one factor inside the accuracy minimums first, then a second factor past them, searched all the way to the validity limits (every zone at its own limit, or at a third of the part when it has none - the base's cap does not stop it), each solved on the real estimate; both are monotone, so a smaller count never plans more. A count under even the validity limits gets that plan, said as "the lowest this part can go is about N". A count or a size is never a failed run. |
| PLAN-fea-31 | in force | **The estimate counts what the mesher builds at coarse sizes, and the count is checked on the built mesh** (`engines/fea_size_field.elements`, `engines/fea_driver.reaim_factor`). The size field reads the surface as well as the volume: every surface triangle's size and its face's measured wall, as (size, wall, area) rows; the estimate adds per area / h^2 the layer every surface carries (`SURFACE_PER_AREA`), or on a wall thinner than the size what the wall holds past its share of the volume (`SLAB_PER_AREA` 3.7 - a 1 mm plate at 6 mm elements built 523, the volume alone counted 48), whichever is more. Before (#417's lab) the built count overshot the plan by 25% on the customer's assembly asked for a third of its count and by 38% on the textbook groove. A pass of a plan sized to the user's count that builds a count further than 10% from what they asked is planned again once with the measured built over planned (0.6 of it, in log: the full ratio overshot as often as it landed) and built again - once, and never when the miss is the validity limit (the lowest valid plan) - and of the valid builds the one closer to the ask is delivered; a rebuild that fails (even a memory kill) costs nothing, the first build stands. Within 10% is the goal, never a stop: the result's line always says asked, planned and built, and "not reached" with why when it was not. |
| PLAN-fea-32 | in force | **An area inside a part the user sized wins over its part on its faces** (`engines/fea_plan.user_sized`, NOTE-6). Every zone on the area's faces (a hole's, a fillet's, a wall's) is held inside the area's range - finer than asked only where coarser elements cannot be built valid (PLAN-fea-30) - and the area gets a zone of its own at the middle of the range, held 3 sizes off its faces and then growing by PLAN-fea-6. An area sets no base: a coarser size on an area than its part's own cannot coarsen it past the part (the size field takes the finer). "Everything else" sizes the rest of the parts, never an area. The words name the area as the user would ("2 holes, 2 mm across"). Lab (6 Nov, gpt-5.6-terra): a bracket and "0.5 mm at the two small holes, 3 mm elsewhere" - the two 2 mm holes mapped to their area at 0.5 mm, the rest of the part 3 mm; planned 3,455, built 4,267, valid on the first pass. |
| PLAN-fea-33 | in force | **A size the notes ask for the WHOLE mesh is held like the sizes per part** (`engines/fea_requirements.whole_sizes`, NOTE-7). On every part the user did not size themselves (PLAN-fea-29 comes first), a global size G is the part's own size and no zone is held finer than their shortest edge (half of G when they gave none); a longest edge alone brings the rules' base down to it and holds nothing up; a shortest edge alone holds every zone over it and leaves the base the rules' own. The plan then treats it as PLAN-fea-29 and PLAN-fea-30 treat any size the user asked for: past the accuracy minimums and said, never past what builds valid, the rules' own plan when no pass of it builds (said). The plan's words say it once: "You asked for 5-15 mm elements on the whole mesh: they are meshed at 10 mm ...". The solid part of a mixed mesh takes it the same way. |
| PLAN-fea-34 | in force | **Every requirement of the notes is told asked against delivered on the built solid mesh** (`engines/fea_requirements.report`, measured by `engines/gmsh/fea_adapter.py` into `quality.json`). Sizes: the typical edge, and how many elements and what percent have an edge shorter or longer than asked. Quality limits a tetrahedron has a figure for - aspect ratio (longest edge over shortest), Jacobian (smallest over largest determinant at the element's nodes), the faces' corner angles - are measured against their target, allowed share and outer limit, and reported, never promised. An aspect ratio or a corner-angle limit is also a TARGET of the quality pass (MESH-mmg-1, rulebook 67): elements past it are reshaped inside the volume where that is possible with the surface mesh frozen, and the row says what the pass did ("the quality pass reshaped the inside of the volume around them: 40 past it before, 12 after"); a Jacobian limit is not (a straight tetrahedron's Jacobian ratio is 1: it changes only as the sides curve). Warpage, skew and taper (four-sided elements), a wall thickness, growth, linear elements and shell element kinds are listed as not applied, with the reason. Each measured requirement is a row of the one list the result screen and the verdict read (`engines/mesh_requirements.asked_rows`: the elements outside what was asked, 0 when met), in its own plain line ("Aspect ratio under 4: not fully met - 2.1% of elements (312) are over 4; the worst is 7.3."); one not reached is a note - the verdict is passed with notes - never a blocker. The closing message carries one short line of counts by outcome ("From your notes: Of the 10 things you asked for: 8 met, 1 not fully met; 1 edge-length target.", `mesh_requirements.summary`), then every ask in turn, where it came from, its outcome and what was built ("Aspect ratio under 4 (from your notes): not fully met - 2.1% of elements (312) are over 4; the worst is 7.3.", `mesh_requirements.closing`), kept whole (`contracts/user_notes.record_remark` `whole`): the result shows the count line and, under Details, the whole of it. Before, the line carried the first misses with their figures and was cut at the remark limit of 400 characters mid-sentence (live, 10 asks: "... an edge longer tha"). |
| PLAN-fea-35 | in force | **Only parts that touch are fused, each fuse has a time limit, and what runs out is tied** (rulebook 72, `cad/assembly_join.py`, `cad/bounded_steps.py`). The general fuse of every part at once (PLAN-fea-17) cuts each part against every other, on one thread, and cannot be stopped from inside: a turbocharger of ten parts sat in it about five hours on "Measuring your part" (lab, 16-17 Nov), though each pair of its parts that touch fuses in under six seconds - two parts that do not touch at all (an inlet and a mount 0.1 mm apart) were still past 300 s fused together. So WHICH PARTS TOUCH is measured first: two parts whose boxes meet within twice the fuse's fuzzy distance and both parts' tolerances are measured by OpenCASCADE's exact distance between the two solids, on every core (0 when one lies inside the other); a check that runs out of its 60 s counts as touching (the safe side). EACH CONNECTED GROUP of touching parts is fused on its own, and a part touching nothing is left as it is - parts of different groups are further apart than anything the fuse merges, so the groups' fuses are the single fuse: the same shared faces from the same faces of the same parts (the assembly corpus, 17 Nov). EACH FUSE RUNS IN A CHILD PROCESS under a limit of 120 s and 0.25 s a face of the parts it fuses, at most 600 s (the corpus's own fuses take 0.03 to 4 s), killed when it runs out. A group that runs out, or fails, is fused PAIR BY PAIR, each touching pair under its own limit; the pairs that fused make smaller groups, each fused again under its limit (a group of two is its pair's fuse). What still cannot be fused is left apart: in a bonded mesh those parts are meshed on their own and TIED where they meet (the tie of PLAN-fea-18, its faces from the pair's own fuse when that finished, else measured on their surfaces), the rest still one shared mesh; when no fuse finished at all, the whole assembly is meshed apart and tied (PLAN-fea-17's way on). The user is told in plain words which parts and why ("Two parts could not be joined in the CAD in the time set for it, so they are meshed apart and tied where they meet: <a> and <b>."), and a pair whose joint nothing found is said, never dropped quietly. The whole join is held to 1800 s; past it nothing more is fused and the rest is tied - a run never waits on a fuse without a bound and never fails only because a fuse was slow. The run shows the join as its own step ("Joining the parts"). The decision record keeps every group, pair and regroup with its faces, limit, time and outcome, and the pairs tied instead; the sidecar keeps the same (`join`). The check's assembly record (the stage) joins the same way. |
| PLAN-fea-36 | in force | **"Finer" or "coarser" on a whole part, with no number, is applied** (rulebook 73, `engines/fea_part_sizes.resolve_scales`). The parts reader (NOTE-4) already read "coarser on the frame" or "refine the bracket" and the request card showed "(coarser there)", but the solid plan kept only phrases with a size, so the ask was dropped. Now such a part's own size is halved (finer) or doubled (coarser) - the same one step the flow route's part levels take (PLAN-cfd-4) - from the whole mesh's size when the notes give one (PLAN-fea-33), else the rules' size for that part; it reaches the plan as a size band (PLAN-fea-29) whose lower end is the finest size the rules already give in that part, so nothing in it is raised: walls, rounds and hot spots keep the sizes their accuracy needs on a coarser part, and what was already finer stays on a finer one. A part with a number of its own keeps its number. An area inside a part (NOTE-6) asked "finer" with no number ("make the mesh finer at the fillets") is made finer than the mesh AROUND it (rulebook 92, reading A - Areen, 25 Nov: "go with A, finer than the mesh around it"; `fea_part_sizes._finer_area`): the target is half the size round it - the user's own size for that part or for the whole mesh when they typed one, else the plan's size for the part. The size already there is, face by face, the finest the accuracy rules give it (a hot spot's, a round's, a wall's), else the size round it. Every face already at or under the target: the area KEEPS the rules' sizes - nothing on it is refined, by this rule or by the AI (AI-fea-1), no size for the whole mesh raises it, it gets no zone of its own - and the user is told: "You asked for finer elements at the fillets: the mesh there is already about 1 mm, finer than half of your 3 mm, so it keeps that size. Give a size if you want it finer still." Otherwise each zone wholly on it coarser than the target is brought to it, what is finer keeps its size, and the area gets a zone of its own at the target ("... the mesh there is now about 0.5 mm, half of your 1 mm; what was already finer there keeps its size."). Only a number goes under the rules' sizes: a size typed at the place ("0.2 mm at the fillets") wins as PLAN-fea-32 has it, however small, and "finer" said twice is still the one halving against the surroundings (there is no bigger factor). The cast knuckle (25 Nov): "3 mm elements overall" and "finer at the fillets" took its 21 fillet hot spots from 0.47 mm to 0.14 mm (the AI's 10 per quarter-turn, then that halved) and the mesh from about 1 to 10 million elements; the fillets now keep 0.47 mm (the test bracket: 36,157 planned elements before, 9,624 now, the rules' own 9,635). What came of each place (`fea_part_sizes.finer_outcomes`: the target, the size there, changed or not, the line) is said at "Designing the mesh" (a remark of its own, `fea_finer:<id>`), in the plan's words, in the asked-against-built row (PLAN-fea-34: "kept at the 0.47 mm the accuracy rules give there, already finer than half of your 3 mm; built about ... mm there; give a size to go finer"), and to the plan step (`fea_plan_options.requirements_cost`'s "finer"). "Coarser" at an area keeps what the accuracy rules make finer there; a zone only partly on a sized area keeps its own size (round 7: fea0254's 16 rounds, 289k planned elements, came to 1.9 million; holding the walls on them to the round's 0.028 mm made 124 million). The plan's words say it: "You asked for coarser elements on the frame: its own size is doubled, from 4 mm to 8 mm; walls, rounds and hot spots keep the sizes their accuracy needs." ON A CHANGE REQUEST (VER-9, rulebook 98; `fea_part_sizes._changed_area`) the reference is the mesh the person is looking at, not the mesh round the place: the note the change path writes carries the finished run it was asked on (`change_of`, carried by `mesh_requirements.bind_picks` and `part_words.with_picks` to the picked area), and "finer" at its faces targets half the size the finished run had there, "coarser" twice it - that run's plan at those faces, rebuilt from the same payload (the rules' zones held to the user's other sizes as that run held them, `fea_plan.user_sized`; face by face the finest zone, else the part's size; the coarsest marked face sets the target). Every zone on the area coarser than the target is brought to it (asked coarser: a finer one is raised to it), what is finer keeps its size, the area gets a zone of its own at the target, and nothing else in the plan changes. A typed size still wins. No accuracy rule sets a finer bound of its own; the plan's element limit (PLAN-fea-8) is the only one, and the asked-against-built row says what was built. Told: "You asked for finer elements at <the faces>: the last mesh had about X mm there; this one has about Y mm." and in the asked-against-built row "made about Y mm there, half the X mm the last mesh had there; built about Z mm there". Before (26 Nov, live, 3 mm overall, one mark on a small hole, "make the mesh finer around this hole only, nowhere else"): "the mesh there is already about 0.13-0.25 mm, finer than half of your 3 mm, so it keeps that size" - reading A against the surroundings, and the rules' sizes rather than the ones the last mesh had (the test bracket: the fillet was 1.05 mm by its rules, 1.5 mm in the 3 mm run; now 0.75 mm, 6,230 planned elements to 45,314). The plan step's reading A is unchanged. The FEA shell's place sizes (`engines/shell_places.plan`, SHMESH-52) already halve or double each face's own planned size, the same reference. |
| PLAN-fea-13 | in force | **A progress watchdog, and a budget from the estimate.** A gmsh FEA pass runs until it is done or its budget (LAD-fea-15, rulebook 49) runs out. It is stopped earlier only when it has made no progress for the stall window (`gmsh_runner.FEA_STALL_WINDOW_S`, 900 s: no new mesher output, no new step in `fea_stage.json`, and no CPU burned or memory changed by the mesher's processes - `sandbox/liveness`; gmsh meshes a big assembly's curves for long stretches without printing a line, dev-areen 25 Oct), or at the hard ceiling of the machine it runs on - the Cloud Run mesh job's own limit (`MESH_TIMEOUT_SECONDS`, less a margin), or 20 hours on a VM (RUN-5) - and never at what the attempt or the pipeline has left: the pipeline's deadline moves on while the mesher shows progress (`application/pipeline_budget.progress_extension`: the deadline becomes the stall window plus two heartbeats after the last progress), so only a run with no progress for that long is ended by the clock (dev-areen 25 Oct: a pass still meshing was cut at 110 minutes and its retry given 1 minute). A driver's attempt is likewise judged by the pipeline's time, never the model loop's budget. The adapter notes each step (sizing, surfaces, volume, optimising, curving, writing) and re-notes its own Python steps every 30 s; the live line reads the step. The planned time (elements at the slow fill rate of the part's complexity class: simple 1,500, moderate 1,100, complex 1,000 elements a second) is kept in the why record for reference only, and no plan is cut down for time - only the job's memory lowers the element limit (PLAN-fea-16). A stop is told as ours (run_infrastructure) with what the watchdog saw: how long it ran, the longest silence, and whether it was stuck or hit the ceiling (`engines/gmsh/gmsh_runner._run_fea_watched`, `sandbox/safe_exec.run_watched`). Planning measures its size-field histogram on the job's cores (bit-identical to one core) and finds the first floor that fits by halving the list of floors (24 Oct: the 114-part caldera planned 14 histograms, 35 minutes). gmsh uses every core the job has for its own parallel work; the curves are meshed on one thread and the surfaces on every core by MESH-23 (gmsh's own threaded versions are not repeatable: lab, 24 Oct, 0 of 17 the same twice). |
| PLAN-fea-15 | in force | **The staged solid is read as gmsh's own B-rep.** The staging writes the metre-scaled solid twice: as STEP, and as gmsh's own B-rep file beside it (`geometry.brep`). The FEA path measures and meshes the B-rep when it is there: gmsh's STEP writer and reader together lose faces on some parts (an ANSI bolt could not be read back; an ISO 4762 screw left a face with no surface mesh). A solid the measurement still cannot read is healed (OpenCASCADE's ShapeFix) and measured again, with a note, so the part still gets a plan. |
| PLAN-fea-11 | in force | **Curving never makes an element worse.** An element that curving onto the CAD leaves under the quality floor and worse than its straight shape keeps straight sides (its midside nodes at the middle of its edges; `engines/gmsh/fea_settle.py`). **Settled to the end:** a straightened element straightens its neighbours on the edges they share, and a neighbour left half straight, half curved can fold, so the check runs again on the elements sharing a moved node until none is left under the floor and worse than straight (`fea_settle.MAX_ROUNDS` the backstop). dev-areen, 9 Nov: one round straightened 590 elements and left 4 of 6.47M inverted, and the whole mesh was refused; settled to the end, none. ox_mc_lid (lab, 27 Oct) the same: one round straightened 335 elements on two 2 mm plates and left 41 neighbours below zero; the rounds after it straighten them and leave none. |

The numbers are `engines/fea_plan.py` (the limit and the coarsening are `engines/fea_driver.choose_plan`). The estimate is read from the plan's own size field over the
part's volume (`engines/fea_size_field.py`), and the plan records it, so the plan can be worked out
again from what it recorded.

## LAD-fea: the FEA driver's fixed lever ladder

One attempt runs at most 3 passes. After a pass, the first trigger that holds pulls its lever, each
lever at most once. The passes do not use up the run's attempts.

| ID | Status | Rule |
|---|---|---|
| LAD-fea-1 | in force | **An invalid mesh** (a degenerate or inverted element): the next pass grows sizes more gently (1.2) and optimises three times instead of once. Elements under the quality note floor are a note, not a trigger. |
| LAD-fea-2 | in force | **No mesh came back** (the fill failed): the next pass fills with gmsh's other 3D algorithm first (Delaunay first, HXT after it; every pass fills with HXT first by MESH-25). |
| LAD-fea-3 | in force | **A plan rule fell short on the mesh** (through a wall, or round a hot spot): the zones that fell short are made 20% finer - never on a plan coarsened to fit the limit, which is short there by choice and says so. |
| LAD-fea-4 | in force | **More elements than the limit** after meshing (the estimate ran low): the next pass makes the finest zones coarser, aimed at 85% of the limit, never past what keeps elements valid. |
| LAD-fea-6 | in force | **No identical retry.** The surfaces a failed pass went wrong on - the ones the fill named, or the ones an invalid element touches - are meshed the other way (gmsh's MeshAdapt surface algorithm instead of the default) on the next pass - ONLY the surfaces that failed: a failure that names more than a quarter of the part's surfaces (a fill that names a whole volume's boundary) is not on those surfaces, and none is switched; and a large analytic face (plane, cylinder, cone, sphere or torus holding over 5% of the part's surface area) keeps the default, where MeshAdapt is very slow and gmsh switches by itself should the default fail there (shared dev, 1 Nov: all 441 faces of a 640 mm part put on MeshAdapt, which ground on its 180,000 mm2 cylinder for hours; rulebook 49): together with LAD-fea-1 or LAD-fea-2, or on its own when those were pulled already. A surface the default algorithm leaves with no mesh is meshed again with MeshAdapt at once, in the same pass. When there is no surface left to change, the ladder stops rather than run the same pass twice. An element the quality record reads as SICN 0.0 (under 5e-5, the record's four decimals) makes the pass invalid and is traced like an inverted one. **A sliver locked between two faces** (an invalid element whose four corners all lie on two surfaces, none inside: two faces facing each other across a wall one element thick get the same surface mesh, node across from node) names only the second face of the pair, so only that one is meshed the other way and the mirror breaks - every pair alike it (same kind and area, the same distance apart) names its second face too, so one pass breaks every mirror instead of one pane per pass; meshing both the other way mirrors them again (fc_glass_frames' 10 mm pane at 7.5 mm: worst SICN 1.5e-5 with both faces alike, 0.22 with one meshed by MeshAdapt). |
| LAD-fea-7 | in force | **Out of memory: the next machine, then no retry.** A pass killed for memory runs again unchanged one machine up (RUN-3) - no lever, no re-plan. Killed on the largest machine too, it pulls no lever (the other 3D algorithm and gentler growth need as much memory or more); the attempt ends saying the plan needs more memory than the largest machine has. |
| LAD-fea-8 | in force | **A surface stage that stalls is not run again the same way.** When the watchdog (PLAN-fea-13) stops the mesher while gmsh is still in its 2D stage, on surface N, the stall is the part's: gmsh threw away its Frontal-Delaunay mesh of a face because two triangles came out the same, and the MeshAdapt run it switched to can stay silent past the watchdog's window (nakuja_rocket's nose cone alone: 15 minutes on one face with a pole on a laptop-class worker). The next pass keeps gmsh's first mesh of every face (no switch to MeshAdapt), its repeated triangles dropped by MESH-5. The surface it stalled on is never put on MeshAdapt again by an earlier LAD-fea-6, and a pass stopped by its budget in the 2D stage (LAD-fea-15) pulls this lever the same way. **It also rides along after a failed pass in which gmsh switched a face to MeshAdapt by itself** (its default failed there and its switch-on-failure took over: a MeshAdapt line for a face first meshed another way, `fea_ladder.switched_surfaces`): the next pass's fill lever (LAD-fea-2 or -1) keeps gmsh's first mesh of every face, those faces are never put on MeshAdapt by LAD-fea-6, and its words say so - so the pass after a failure is not the same grind again (shared dev, 1 Nov: a face lying on another face spent 13 minutes in MeshAdapt on every pass, and the fill failed each time; rulebook 49). Once per attempt; any other guard trip stays ours and pulls no lever. |
| LAD-fea-9 | in force | **The last rung: fTetWild, only after every gmsh option** (`engines/fea_tetwild.py`). When every pass above - Delaunay, HXT, MeshAdapt on the surfaces that failed, and for an assembly the tie (PLAN-fea-17) - left no valid mesh, fTetWild (wildmeshing/fTetWild, pinned commit, MPL-2.0) meshes the part(s) still without one: an assembly's parts the failed passes named first, tied to the other parts' unchanged gmsh mesh; if that pass fails too, every part. Its ideal edge length is the part's base size and the plan's zones are its sizing field, its envelope a thousandth of the part's diagonal - each held to fTetWild's own sizes by LAD-fea-17; the CAD is tessellated within half the envelope; its time, stop and threads are LAD-fea-18's; the image's build is patched (seeds made constants, thread-stable, a stop file: `deploy/docker/ftetwild_patch.sh`), so the same input gives the same mesh. The tetrahedra get a node at every edge's middle (10-node, valid exactly when the 4-node one is) and run through the same checks, gates and writers. Every boundary triangle is named after the CAD face nearest to it (Face_k / Part_k_Face_j); the delivery says the envelope, the furthest measured distance from the CAD, the faces whose surface moved more than half the envelope and any face too small to keep an element face. Never for a guard trip, a worker too small, or a plan out of memory on the largest machine (LAD-fea-7). |
| LAD-fea-10 | in force | **The last rung's ideal edge is never longer than the part.** A plan coarsened to fit (PLAN-fea-8) floors every part's base at its zone floor, and on a big assembly that floor passes the small parts' own size (ox_mc_lid: 61 mm on parts of 3 to 20 mm). gmsh is held there by each part's face zones; fTetWild, asked for edges twenty times its part inside an envelope a thousandth of it, ran past 37 GB and an hour on a 3.2 mm part and past 41 GB and two hours on a 6 mm one without finishing. So the rung's ideal edge for a part is its base, never more than the part's own diagonal (that part: 0.21 GB, 4.4 minutes, 26,236 tetrahedra; lab, 26 Oct). A part whose base is within its own size - every part of a plan not coarsened that far - is unchanged; the rung's settings say `edge_rule` and the plan's base where it held. |
| LAD-fea-11 | in force | **Parts a failed pass did mesh are kept; later passes mesh only the parts that failed.** Parts meshed apart (tied or contact) are filled one by one; the fill stops at the first part it fails on (after an error gmsh refuses every further mesh call in that process) and the pass keeps the parts it filled - a volume gmsh left empty, or filled with a flat or inverted element (SICN under 5e-5; kept, Netgen's optimiser crashed on one, the folding chair, lab 28 Oct), is a failed part, never a kept one (`fea_kept_parts.msh`: gmsh's own file at the scale the pass built at, with each node's place on its face; `fea_kept_parts.json`: the scale, the sidecar's ties, the kept and failed volumes). The next passes - every lever, and the last rung's first pass - read the kept parts back onto the same CAD entities at that scale and mesh only the others - the parts not reached first, the parts that failed last (their faces with MeshAdapt where LAD-fea-6 says); the rung's first pass gives fTetWild every part not kept - those still failing and those the last gmsh pass never reached, each on its own settings - never a kept one (tried again inside the rung, a part gmsh never reached that failed sent every part to fTetWild on the whole assembly's settings); a kept part's tiny-face merges (MESH-9) travel with its mesh, and its faces are never merged again; the kept parts' meshes go through the same optimiser, curving, checks and writers as every mesh. A pass that meshes every part never writes the file, so every mesh that passes first time is unchanged. A new staging (the tie after a fused pass) starts afresh. ox_mc_lid: pass 1 fills 238 parts in about 5.5 h and stops on Part_239; pass 2 fills the 14 not reached and fails on Part_239 again (about 40 min instead of 5 h), and the rung's pass gives Part_239 alone to fTetWild. |
| LAD-fea-12 | in force | **The last rung's slivers on the surface are taken off, or set aside and named** (27 Oct, `engines/fea_tetwild.peel_flat`, `mesh_into`). fTetWild can leave all-but-flat tetrahedra on the part's surface: volume under 1e-6 of the longest edge cubed (a regular one has 0.118), SICN about 5e-5 and under - what the quality record (four decimals) reads as 0, so the ladder calls the pass invalid. One with two or three faces outside holds next to no material: it is dropped, flattest first, when the surface without it has no more edges off two triangles than it had, and again while dropping one lays bare another. One still under SICN 5e-5 with a face on the surface and the rest inside cannot be dropped: it is set aside from the quality floor, the way an element on a micro detail is (PLAN-fea-12), and each one is named - its part, the CAD face it lies on, its SICN and where (`fea_set_aside.ftetwild_slivers`, the concern `fea_ftetwild_slivers`). Such a mesh is NEVER a clean pass: the concern row is always missed, so the verdict is "with concerns" (VER-2) - a bad element hidden from the user is worse than a failure. Validity still holds it (none may be inverted). A flat one wholly inside is left for the gate. ox_mc_lid (lab, 27 Oct): Part_239's 769,908 tetrahedra had nine such slivers in two spots 20-50 microns across - the flattest measured inside out by gmsh, alone failing the delivery; seven are dropped and two set aside. |
| LAD-fea-13 | in force | **After the last rung, one refinement pass on the parts it left short** (`engines/fea_driver._rung_refine`, `engines/fea_tetwild.refine_targets`). The rung's pass is the ladder's last, so a mesh it delivers short of a rule - a wall under 2 elements through (PLAN-fea-3), a hot spot under its count - had no pass left for LAD-fea-3. Now, when the rung's pass is valid and short (and the plan was not coarsened to fit, where short is by choice), one pass more re-meshes only the parts the short zones are on - a short wall on the part of the face the check measured (zone ids repeat across parts), a hot spot on its own faces' parts - each part's own short zone entries LAD-fea-3 finer (0.8). A part gmsh meshed is meshed by gmsh again (not kept, LAD-fea-11); a part fTetWild meshed is meshed by fTetWild with those zones HELD THROUGH ITS SIZING GRID - a zone's size rises at most (growth - 1) per metre away from it, so each grid node near it reads that much less and no point between the nodes reads coarser than the zone (a uniform grid let walls thinner than a cell fall through). Every other part is as before: a kept gmsh part kept, an fTetWild part whose run has the same inputs taken from its folder (one thread, constant seeds: the same mesh). The machine holds twice the rung's measured mesh at the least (RUN-6). A mesh is already in hand, so the refinement takes at most twice the rung pass's own time (10 minutes at the least) and never moves to a bigger machine or a longer wall (RUN-3/RUN-5 do not apply to it): out of time or memory, it ends. Its fTetWild parts are asked to finish the mesh they have inside that time (LAD-fea-18), with the rung pass's own sizes (LAD-fea-17), and no gmsh pass's fill budget (LAD-fea-15) is left to stop it: the rung and the refinement are timed by their own budgets. The better of the two passes is delivered - valid, within the limit, fewer zones short, or as many with the worst wall holding more elements - the other marked not deliverable; the delivery names every wall still short: its face, zone, thickness and elements through (the concern `fea_walls_short`, never a clean pass). STEP hunt, 27 Oct: five assemblies were delivered by the rung with walls at 1.4-1.8 elements; a finer grid everywhere fixed one and ran two out of memory. |
| LAD-fea-14 | in force | **A 3D refinement that spins is a fill that failed.** gmsh's Delaunay refinement can keep inserting points and rejecting nearly all of them; it prints and burns CPU the whole time, so the progress watchdog (PLAN-fea-13) never stops it (g_c0103, a 9-part gripper mount planned at 1.4M elements: 3.8 hours to the lab's limit; after 8 minutes, 9.3M iterations had kept 211k nodes and rejected 8.9M points, where a healthy refinement rejects none). The watchdog reads gmsh's own counts ("It. N - M nodes created ... (nodes removed a b)") at every look: past 1,000,000 iterations, rejected points over 10 times the nodes kept, held for 5 minutes, and the pass is stopped (`gmsh_runner.RefineSpin`). That is the part's, never ours: the pass counts as a fill with no mesh, the ladder goes on as after any failed fill (LAD-fea-2: HXT next; LAD-fea-9 when every option is spent), and the decision record says what gmsh's counts were. A refinement that keeps its points is never stopped by this. |
| LAD-fea-15 | in force | **A pass gets the time its size allows, then the ladder goes on** (`engines/fea_driver.pass_work`, rulebook 49). A pass's budget is 5 times its planned time (its estimate at the part's slow fill rate, PLAN-fea-13), at least one hour, at most the wall of the machine it runs on. A pass still working when its budget runs out is the part's slowness, never ours: it is read as a fill that failed, cited with its planned time and the surface it was on, and the ladder goes on - stopped in the 2D stage, LAD-fea-8 first; then the other 3D algorithm (LAD-fea-2); when every gmsh option is spent, fTetWild (LAD-fea-9). It is never moved to a VM to grind on (RUN-5 moves only a pass the Cloud Run wall stopped, when its budget reached past that wall). **The budget only ever stops a fill.** It rides in the workspace (`fea_budget.json`) to the machine the mesher runs on, where the watchdog (`gmsh_runner.FillBudget`) applies it only while the mesher's step is sizing, surfaces or volume; once the solid is filled, improving, curving, checking and writing a mesh that exists are stopped only by the stall watch or the machine's wall, which is the mesher's own clock (dev-areen, 9 Nov: a 2.4M-element helmet filled in 3 hours on a VM started with a 320-minute limit, and its clean-up was due to be cut with nothing delivered). Calibration: Calibrated on the 260-part corpus run on this code (lab, 1 Nov): the slowest pass that built a mesh took 2.6 times its planned time at 1,500 elements a second (median 0.52, 99th percentile 1.9), so 5 times the planned time at the part's own, slower rate cuts none of them.. Shared dev, 1 Nov: a pass with a 20-hour ceiling ground in MeshAdapt on one face for over an hour. |
| LAD-fea-16 | in force | **A fill that failed on CAD faces already found crossing goes straight to the last rung** (`engines/fea_driver.crossing_located`, rulebook 49). When a pass builds no mesh and, before meshing, the geometry check confirmed faces of the CAD crossing each other (a self-overlap both its measures agree on, `cad/suspect_defects`) or the repair inspection found a self-intersection (`pipeline/repair_inspect`), no gmsh algorithm swap is spent on it: LAD-fea-1, -2 and -6 cannot mend a crossing in the CAD. The ladder ends there and fTetWild meshes the part (LAD-fea-9), which meshes through such a crossing within its envelope; a bonded assembly is first tied, as PLAN-fea-17 says, and goes to the rung if that fails too. The why record and the plan's words say which check found which faces. Shared dev, 1 Nov: job bc3d4271 (an IGES landing gear, 3 crossing faces found by the repair inspection) spent 29 minutes on pass 1, then put all 567 faces on MeshAdapt. Corpus: it changes no corpus run - the only corpus part whose fill failed (fea0222) has a fold, not a crossing, and gmsh's HXT meshes it on pass 2, as before; folds are deliberately left to the ladder. |
| LAD-fea-17 | in force | **The last rung takes fTetWild's own sizes, from the part's real walls** (`engines/fea_tetwild.params`, `real_walls`, `walls_with_room`, rulebook 50). fTetWild's envelope and edges are shares of its own box, and its time grows steeply as the envelope shrinks (lab, 2 Nov, 9 corpus parts on one thread, no sizing field: against a thousandth of the diagonal, 5e-4 took 2.6 times as long, 3e-4 5.5 times, 2e-4 9 times (at worst 15), 1e-4 40 times and 3 of 7 ran past 15 minutes; the sizing field's rounds, up to 20 passes over the refined mesh, took another 1.3 to 15 times). So its envelope is a thousandth of the part's diagonal, and a thin wall lowers it to a tenth of that wall only when the wall is real: a reading on a face the checks found crossing or overlapping another (LAD-fea-16's faces, every self-overlap the geometry check located) does not count, nor one on a face narrower than 3 walls (its area under (3 t)^2: a sliver's local reading), and a reading that would lower the envelope must have room beyond it - from up to 48 points of its face, one and a half walls in, at least one must be outside the material, by the winding number of the CAD's surface (two faces overlapping inside the material have none: a pad laid 0.5 mm into a bar, never fused). The first real reading, thinnest first, sets the envelope. The envelope is never under 3e-4 of the diagonal unless a real wall holds it lower, and then never past 0.4 of that wall (under half the wall its two faces stay apart), and never under 2e-5 of the diagonal. The sizing field is the plan's zones, none finer than 4 envelopes (fTetWild's surface is only known to its envelope, and its shortest edge is the envelope), and none drawn from a reading with no room beyond it. The ideal edge is LAD-fea-10's. **Material left out is filled again**: where the CAD's surface lies more than 2% of the part from fTetWild's mesh (on unsewn pieces laid into each other its winding number can drop a whole piece), the part runs again with fTetWild's flood fill (what the CAD's surface encloses, from the outside in) within the same time, and the closer mesh is kept, said. The why record names every reading set aside and why; the delivery says the faces whose "walls" were overlaps. Shared dev, 1 Nov: the 640 mm part's rung ran with a 13 micron envelope (a 0.11 mm reading on a 0.04 mm2 sliver face): 80 minutes to insert its 731,000 triangles, still on round 9 after 3 hours; its envelope is now 0.44 mm (a tenth of its first real wall) and fTetWild meshes it in 27 minutes on 8 cores (1.86M quadratic tetrahedra, worst SICN 0.27). A pivot-like lab part (pieces laid into each other, never fused, loose faces): its sizing field asked for 21 micron elements and grew the mesh to 18.7M tetrahedra over 97 minutes, and its bar was left out (55 mm of CAD with no mesh); now 4 minutes, the bar filled again. |
| LAD-fea-18 | in force | **The last rung finishes in bounded time, says where it is, and uses every core** (`engines/fea_tetwild.rung_budget`, `own_budget`, `run_part`, `deploy/docker/ftetwild_patch.sh`, rulebook 50). Its time is 5 times its planned time - the tetrahedra fTetWild's own sizes ask for (the part's volume at the field's density) at fTetWild's measured rate on the job's cores (350 a second on one thread, times the threads to the power 0.25: 8 threads ran 1.6-1.7 times as fast as one; set so the slowest corpus part took 2 times its planned time) - at least 30 minutes, never past 4 hours, never past the driver's budget from the plan's estimate, which sits within the machine's wall with a margin for gmsh's other parts and the files. At 80% of it - or sooner, while three of its longest rounds still fit (the stop is read between rounds) - a part still improving its elements is asked, through the build's stop file, to finish the mesh it has: fTetWild ends its rounds where it is, removes the outside and writes it, and that mesh goes through every check like any other, said as stopped after N rounds. At the whole of it a part still running is stopped and the reason said in plain words (what it was doing, after how long); no second rung pass follows, and the pass is never moved to a VM to grind on (RUN-5 moves only a pass the Cloud Run wall stopped). fTetWild's own stops stand (--stop-energy 10, --max-its 80). Its rounds, worst element energy and element count go to the run's live line as they happen. It runs on every core of the job: one part alone gets them all as TBB threads, parts at once share them; the image's build is patched so the same input gives the same mesh on any number of threads (the cut tets the threads gather taken in index order, the smoothing's parallel sets fixed): the same mesh, to the byte, on 1, 4 and 8 threads. A mesh fTetWild was asked to finish early depends on when the time ran out, so it is the one result that can differ run to run - said as stopped. Corpus (lab, 2 Nov: all 260 corpus parts forced to the rung, two n2-standard-32 VMs, 2 cores a part): 241 delivered before, 254 of 256 after (none lost, 13 gained; the other 2 were stopped by the lab's own 1-hour cap, not by the rung), rung passes past 1 hour 20 before and 7 after, total rung time 36.6 hours before and 20.7 after, median worst SICN 0.397 before and 0.394 after; the stop file finished 2 parts. The user part and the pivot-like part gave the same solver deck twice, and the pivot the same on two machines. |
| LAD-fea-5 | in force | **The best pass is delivered:** a valid mesh first, then every rule met, then within the limit, then the better worst element, then the earlier pass. |

The ladder is `engines/fea_ladder.py`.

## RUN: where a mesh runs

A plan is sized by the accuracy rules alone, then sent where it fits (Areen, 24 Oct: a bigger machine
replaces shrinking a mesh to fit). Engine-neutral: any driver writes the decision into its workspace
(`contracts/mesh_machine.py`, `mesh_machine.json`) and the mesh executor runs it there
(`adapters/mesh_execution/cloud_run_client.py`, `gce_vm.py`).

| ID | Status | Rule |
|---|---|---|
| RUN-1 | in force | **Decided before meshing, from the memory model.** The mesher's peak (the engine's measured model, with its margin) plus the workspace's files (Cloud Run keeps them in memory: about 2,300 bytes an FEA element) is held against the Cloud Run mesh job (32 GiB, 90% usable). It fits: Cloud Run. It does not: the smallest Compute Engine VM whose memory holds the mesher alone (a VM's workspace is on disk) - n2-highmem-8 (64 GB), -16 (128), -32 (256), -64 (512), m1-ultramem-40 (961). A plan that obviously needs a big machine never wastes a first run on Cloud Run. An FEA plan that fits the job's memory but is big runs on more cores anyway (RUN-10), and so does a big flow plan (RUN-13). |
| RUN-2 | in force | **The largest machine is the ceiling** (m1-ultramem-40, about 1 TB). A plan past it runs there, coarsened to fit it - the last resort, said in the plan's words. |
| RUN-3 | in force | **Out of memory: the same plan, one machine up.** A run killed for memory (SIGKILL, exit 137), or stopped by the memory guard before it would be (RUN-5), runs again unchanged on the next machine that also holds the peak seen, until the largest; killed there too, LAD-fea-7 (FEA), or handed back as out of memory (CFD). Every engine, not only FEA. |
| RUN-4 | in force | **The VM is the job.** It runs the Cloud Run mesh job's own image as the job's own service account, through the same runner and exchange (INPUT_URI, OUTPUT_URI, ENGINE, TIMEOUT); its workspace is on disk; the progress watchdog (PLAN-fea-13) runs inside it as on Cloud Run. The worker waits 30 minutes longer for it (boot and image pull), and past that for as long as the remote is still heard from (its beacon pulses every minute while it meshes and while it sends the result back); 20 minutes with no pulse means the remote is gone and ends the wait, whatever the deadline, deletes it once its result is written - collected or not - and finds it by name when another worker started it. **The VM never outlives its run** (28 Oct): it ends itself when its container exits or the run's timeout plus 30 minutes passes, whatever became of the worker - it asks to delete itself, three times, and when that is refused (its identity may not delete instances) it powers off, which needs no permission and stops its billing; and every worker sweeps its deployment's VMs at start and every 15 minutes, deleting one that has stopped, whose result is written, or whose job has ended or is named by no claim (after 30 minutes) - never another deployment's, and never one still WORKING - a VM whose beacon was written within 20 minutes (meshing, sending its result back, or finishing after it) is left alone whatever its claim says, since a claim records its VM only once the run is over (28 Oct: as first merged, the sweep would have deleted every VM run 30 to 45 minutes in, its claim not yet naming it) (`adapters/mesh_execution/vm_sweep.py`). **A job keeps its VM across its remeshes** (19 Nov): a VM is held for the job that started it - named by the job and machine type, labelled `job` - for as long as the job's meshing run holds it. The run (`hold_job_machines`, around the graph) renews the job's hold (`vm-holds/<job>` in the exchange bucket) every minute, and a VM whose run is done waits for the job's next run while the hold is renewed, however long the builder takes - there is no idle timer. A remesh, a rerun or a retry on that machine is handed to it in its metadata (`mesh-run`) and starts at once, with no boot and no image pull (a new image is pulled first). The worker no longer deletes a held VM when its result is written. The job's end releases its VMs at once (`release_job_machines`, called by the owner that finalized it: the hold is deleted, then the VMs). A VM whose hold is gone or not renewed for 5 minutes (its worker died) ends itself. A step to another machine type releases the job's VM on the old type once its run is over. A held VM is handed a run only once its last run is over (its result written, or collected) and while its hold has been unbroken since; one that is stopped, saw its hold lapse, or has too little lifetime left is replaced. A held VM still meshing (a pass left to finish for a job taken over) is never deleted or queued behind: the run gets a VM of its own that ends with it, as does a run whose job's VM name is not yet free. Compute Engine's limit for a held VM is at least 6 hours. The sweep judges a held VM by its job, not by its last result: it deletes the VM once its job has ended, or once its hold has gone unrenewed for 25 minutes. Job 6cb2d606's VM, its 107M-cell result too big to bring back and its own delete refused in silence, billed for 4.5 hours after its run. Compute Engine deletes it at its timeout plus an hour whatever happens. Where each pass ran, and why, is in the why record (`fea_machine`). **A pass our side never ran is ours** (26 Oct): when its machine could not be started (a refused permission, no machine of that size free, a quota), was started but never heard from within 30 minutes (a VM or the mesh job, any engine: it did not boot, or could not pull its image - stopped then, not waited on to its deadline and told as a mesh too big for its time), went silent part way (heard, then nothing for 20 minutes: its machine went away), or its result never came back, no lever is pulled and no other pass, tie or rung is tried - none would run either - and the attempt ends at once as our failure (`run_infrastructure`, gate `fea_dispatch`), said as that and never as the part's geometry. Every worker checks at start, as itself, that it holds what this route calls (run the job, read it, act as its account, create and delete the VM) and logs the command for any it lacks (`adapters/mesh_execution/access_check.py`). **A remote mesh keeps its job, and its work is never thrown away** (28 Oct: the cyclone, job adf9412b, meshed for 7 hours on a VM, lost its lease minutes before its result came back, and the VM was deleted while sending it): every remote run - a VM or the Cloud Run mesh job - says in its beacon when its processes last worked (`alive_at`: CPU, memory, output), and that is the job's progress, so the deadline moves on while it works even when its log stands still; the worker marks every beacon it hears, and the lease keeper renews for a worker that is rightly idle while its remote is heard within 15 minutes (a remote silent that long is still let go). Only the owner's cancel stops a remote run: a job taken over, whose lease lapsed or whose worker is going away leaves it to finish, and one already sending its result back, or whose result is written, is never stopped; a job handed on while its result was collected leaves its exchange for the next owner. A new owner adopts the run an earlier generation of the job started - the same operation, payload and engine - when it is still running or its result is written, and collects it instead of meshing again (`application/native_submission`, `application/lease_keeper`). |
| RUN-5 | in force | **Time: the Cloud Run wall is never what stops a working mesher - any engine.** Cloud Run stops every run of the mesh job at its task timeout (14,400 s), a platform limit; every native run is otherwise stopped only after 15 minutes with no sign of life (no new output, no CPU work, no memory change - `sandbox/liveness`), or at the wall of the machine it is on (`contracts/native_time`: Cloud Run 13,800 s, a VM 20 hours, a backstop under the worker task's own 24 hours). Every CFD engine runs this way: snappy, cfMesh, VMTK, multi-region snappy, gmsh and snap-grid (their old fixed limits - VMTK 3,000 s, multi-region snappy 3,000 s a stage, gmsh 1,500 s, snap-grid 3,600 s - are now only a record: SW-13, which read them, is retired by TIME-1); a run's output is watched even when the engine reads it back whole (`sandbox/safe_exec.run_guarded`). Before meshing, a plan whose planned time times 1.5 passes the Cloud Run wall runs on the smallest VM that holds it (FEA: elements at the part's slow fill rate, PLAN-fea-13; cfMesh: its run plan's estimated cells at its measured rate; multi-region snappy: its thermal plan's cells at its measured rate) - and only a plan past even a VM's wall is made coarser (or, multi-region, refused: its thermal plan was refused past 80% of a fixed 3,000 s). After a run, a pass stopped at the Cloud Run wall still working (not stalled) runs again, the same plan, on that VM - nothing coarsened, no lever (FEA, snappy, and every engine the builder runs or a driver runs watched - cfMesh, VMTK, multi-region snappy, gmsh, snap-grid: `contracts/native_time.run_off_the_wall`). A run that STALLED, or met even a VM's wall, is a guard trip (TIME-3): the attempt is replayed once as it was, then the run ends as ran out of time - never a coarser pass, another engine, or VMTK's layer-free fill (which used to wait while a layered fill got 60% of a fixed 50 minutes). A VMTK ladder step still working at the wall starts no other step, and the whole run goes to the VM. SIZE, NOT TIME, for VMTK's layers too: a layered fill whose own output shows the layer generator's final warp tangled the layer again more than once (it prints "try last" before each final warp; a layer that converges prints it once, and the rounds otherwise go on to 200 at 100% of a core - the Fluent aorta, 4 in 2.5 hours) is stopped there (`engines/vmtk/vmtk_runner.UntangleBrake`). When the plan asks for wall layers and another engine is left, the run moves straight to it as soon as the layer needs untangling at all ("untangle procedure": of 706 VMTK passes whose log shows the layer stage, none ever needed it, and every run that did failed) ("VMTK layer needed untangling - no passing layered VMTK run ever did (0 of 706) - next engine"); only when the plan asks for none, or no later engine is left, does the ladder run VMTK's layer-free fill (the Fluent aorta: a layer-free VMTK mesh in 2.4 hours, where snappy then built 99% layers in 1.2). gmsh's CFD fill is sized the same way: its passage-sized fill (13 cells across every passage) is estimated on the coarse fill the passage field is measured on, and a fill past 2 times the job's cell limit is never started - the run moves to the next engine at once (`engines/gmsh/driver.estimate_fill`; the shell side of a 19-tube heat exchanger grew past 4 million nodes and was killed for memory at 33 GB after 3.8 hours). NEVER AN OUT-OF-MEMORY KILL: every watched run is stopped, cleanly, once what cannot be reclaimed on its machine passes 92% of what the machine allows (`sandbox/liveness.memory_in_use`), and the same case runs again one machine up (RUN-3, now every watched engine, not only FEA: `contracts/native_time.run_off_the_wall`); on the largest machine it is handed back as out of memory. The VM run is a submission of its own (the machine joins its identity). Native mesher time never spends the builder loop's model budgets (per attempt or per run). SIZE, NOT TIME: a snappy pass on an external body whose own printed count passes the plan's cell budget by its margin - the castellated count 1.5 times, the count with every layer (printed before the layer stage runs, and above what the layers really add) 2 times - is stopped there and re-planned with the castellation budget cut, by at most half a pass (the budget is what the case was priced at, and a stop the re-plan cannot answer is released: RUN-8) (`engines/size_brake`; from 71 lab runs: within-plan runs read at most 1.18x castellated, a production-grade plate 1.70x with its layers, overshoots 3.2x and up); a plan whose estimate with its layers passes 1.5 times the plan gets a castellation budget leaving room for them, never under a quarter of the plan. cfMesh's run is braked the same way on the count it prints after its octree. |
| RUN-6 | in force | **The last rung's machine from fTetWild's own measured memory.** The rung's pass (LAD-fea-9) holds gmsh's model of every part - the gmsh memory model of RUN-1, with its margin - while fTetWild runs one part per CPU beside it. fTetWild's own model, measured part by part on the lab with each part's own peak (26 Oct, 23 of ox_mc_lid's parts at LAD-fea-10 edges, 1,090 to 2.39M tetrahedra: 0.19 to 3.1 GB): 0.25 GB per running part plus 2.1 GB per million tetrahedra (the steepest part measured), with RUN-1's margin, for as many parts at once as the machine has CPUs (the Cloud Run job: 8). The smallest place holding both runs the pass; the decision records both parts of the need. Each part's recorded peak is its own high-water mark (VmHWM, read while it runs): the reaped rusage carries the starting process's memory across fork and exec, so beside a 1.5 GB parent every small part read 1.5 GB. |
| RUN-7 | in force | **A big result is packed where it was made; its delivery-only files stay in the bucket; what comes home is sized by what was built.** The worker that collects a run is small (dev: 4 CPUs, 16 GB, about 90 GB of disk). On the mesher, before the workspace is archived, every file of 64 MiB or more goes to the exchange bucket on its own, already packed as the delivery bundle holds it - gzip members of its tar data at level 1, compressed on all the mesher's CPUs - and leaves the archive, which then carries only the small files (the worker used to compress the bundle itself: 93 minutes at level 9 for job 6cb2d606's 22 GB polyMesh). A file the worker's own checks read (a flow mesh's polyMesh) is fetched: unpacked to its place at collection. Each solver deck (`mesh.inp`, `.bdf`, `.cdb`, `.unv`) and each flow mesh solver file (`export_openfoam_case.zip`, `export_fluent.zip`, `export_ensight.zip`, `engines/cfd_exports.py`) of 256 MiB or more, and gmsh's own mesh (`mesh.msh`) of an FEA plan, is kept in the bucket instead; the worker only ever asks whether these are there. The bundle's big members - kept, or fetched and unchanged since - pass through as the mesher packed them; one the worker changed is packed again. Before gmsh's mesh goes, the mesher - which has the memory - writes the two light copies the worker reads in its place: the viewer's surface (`viewer_mesh.npz`, thinned face by face to at most 1.5M triangles with every face's outline kept) and a surface-only mesh with every named face for the reviewer (`review_surface.msh`, which the manifest then names). A record of what stayed (`kept_in_bucket.json`) rides home; the worker refuses one that names any object but this run's own. Delivery streams every download from the bucket into the artifact store - the solver files, gmsh's mesh, and the bundle, whose kept members pass through as stored - so none touches the worker's disk; the bundle is the same archive, byte for byte once unzipped, as one built from local files. The objects are deleted once the delivery is committed (the bucket's two-day lifecycle backs this up). An FEA pass kept for later shares its mesh files with the workspace (hard links), never a second copy. **Every result, flow or FEA, is collected under limits sized from what the mesher measured it sent** (the archive and the expanded total at 1.15 times its raw bytes, the largest file at 1.15 times its own, never past 48 GiB), and only when 85% of the worker's free disk holds it twice over (the archive, then the bundle) plus its files - else it is kept in the exchange for a worker with room, and said so. A cfMesh plan's machine is sized by the files its estimated cells will write (400 bytes a cell). Job 6cb2d606's 107M-cell cfMesh mesh (3.4 GB packed, 22 GB raw) was lost to the old cell-capped flow allowance (3.2 GB packed, 12.8 GB raw) after 44 minutes of meshing (`engines/bucket_files.py`, `adapters/mesh_execution/cloud_run_client.result_extraction_limits`). |
| RUN-8 | in force | **The size brake measures what the case was priced at, and only stops what it can make smaller.** The budget a snappy pass is braked against (RUN-5) is the plan's own cell budget plus the cells the gap boxes between an external body's parts raised snappy's ceiling by (PLAN-cfd-1: 13 cells across every gap, priced into maxGlobalCells by `snappy_runner._plan_body_gaps`) - those cells are asked for on purpose, so they are never read as a runaway (a slotted wing planned at 2M and raised to 6.44M for its gaps was stopped at 4.97M castellated cells and ended with no mesh; without the brake it meshed production-grade at 6.24M). A plan the gap boxes did not raise is braked exactly as before. And when the pass after a brake stop authors the very case the brake stopped, byte for byte (nothing left to cut: the gap boxes, the background mesh or the budget's floor price it back in), that case runs once more with the brake released (`engines/size_brake.release_brake`) - a run is never ended with no mesh for its size alone; the release is recorded in the why record with the count, budget and pass. **Every pass is braked against the first pass's budget**: a plan that names no budget of its own (the rules plan) is measured against the budget its first pass was built with, never the one a stop has just cut - RM5 read 2.9x of 4 M, then 5.2x of 2 M and 4.5x of 1 M, eight passes stopped and a wrapped 0.81 M mesh after 168 minutes. **A stopped pass teaches the re-plan what its wall holds** (`engines/size_brake.learned_face_factor`): a stop on the with-layers count carries the castellated count beside it, so the wall's faces are measured - (with layers - castellated) / layers - against the area over the wall cell squared the plan priced them at (on RM5 0.50 M against at most 0.15 M: its sharp edges are refined two levels finer, sixteen faces to one). The re-plan's estimate with its layers (`snappy_runner.final_estimate`, `face_factor`) works from that measured wall, at least 1 and at most 25 times the priced one, so a body at the coarsest wall that seals it is planned at what it needs (rulebook 75) from the second pass on. That pricing holds whatever the notes ask, except a cell count the user set (CFDREQ-3); it had been off for any request at all, RM5's "OK" wall treatment included. |
| RUN-9 | in force | **A flow mesh's cell count picks its machine; the count is the user's** (`contracts/mesh_machine.for_cells` / `route_cells`; Areen, 14 Nov: "if the person writes 16 M in the notes, okay, move to the big machine; if they say 5 M, we don't; if they don't say anything, just mesh how we normally mesh"). A plan of up to `settings/policy.CELL_HARD_LIMIT` cells (8 M) runs on the standard machine, as always; a plan past it - on snappyHexMesh and cfMesh only ever the user's own count (CFDREQ-3), since our budgets stop at the standard machine - is written, before anything is meshed and with no question, to run on the smallest VM whose memory holds the mesher at `FLOW_GIB_PER_MCELL` (2.5 GiB a million cells, an estimate; RUN-3 steps a run that still needs more one machine up). A bigger machine already decided (a step up) is kept. The largest machine carries about 345 M cells; a count past it is the nearest, said. A sound mesh that comes out over the standard machine's cells is delivered as it is (LAD-cfd-4) - never thrown away to be re-meshed smaller - and its result is brought home at its own size, bounded by what the largest machine carries (`cloud_run_client.result_extraction_limits`). |
| RUN-11 | in force | **Every FEA solid run says where its time went** (rulebook 99, `engines/fea_times.py`; the owner, 26 Nov: a 41-minute run whose report could not say where the minutes went). Each step the run takes is logged once, on the clock the stage file already carries (`engines/gmsh/fea_adapter.note_stage`'s `at`; no step is timed twice): the driver's own (measuring, planning, each mesher run, delivering) and the mesher's (the sizes, the surfaces, the fill with its algorithm and threads, the smoothing pass and the quality pass with what each changed, curving, the check of the curved elements, the solver files). A second mesh made to compare (MESH-24) or a fill made again another way (MESH-25) is one step of its own. The sums go into the run's record (`fea_times.json`, the result block's `times`) and the report's small table "Where the time went", in plain words; the closing message says one line only when one step took more than half the run ("Most of the time, 24 of 41 min, went to ..."). quality.json carries no time: the same mesh still gives the same record. |
| RUN-10 | in force | **A big FEA mesh runs on more cores, even when it fits the Cloud Run job's memory** (`contracts/mesh_machine.for_time` with the plan's elements; Areen, 26 Nov: "cost is not an issue, give it more cores if we can"). A plan of at least `SPEED_ELEMENTS` (300,000) planned elements runs on `SPEED_MACHINE` (c2d-standard-32: compute-optimised, 32 vCPUs = 16 cores, 128 GB), kept apart from the memory ladder (`MACHINES`, which memory alone picks from); below it, the Cloud Run job (8 cores) as before - a small mesh is done before a VM has booted (about 90 s). Measured on the owner's 857,000-element solid: 41 minutes on the 8-core job, 7 minutes (nothing typed) and 14.6 minutes (his requirements) on 16 laptop threads. **Why c2d-standard-32** (26 Nov, real VMs, the owner's part, ~775,000-795,000 second-order tetrahedra, 3.5 GB peak; feabench_lab/BENCH.md): only the HXT fill gains from threads (180 s at 8, 106 at 16, 83 at 32, slower at 64), so the whole run is 426 s on 8 n2 threads, 354 on 16, 331 on 32, 487 on 64; c2d's fill is 10-15% quicker than n2's at equal threads (with typed sizes and finer holes 599 s against 671 at 32); measure+plan (~85-90 s), surfaces, improving (~70 s), curving (~12 s) and check+write (~50 s) do not change with the machine; and 3.5 GB needs no highmem. The memory rule (RUN-1) wins when the mesher needs more than the speed machine's usable memory (115 GiB): the ladder's machine (n2-highmem-32 and up); RUN-3 steps up from the speed machine to the next ladder machine with more memory (n2-highmem-32). The surface workers (MESH-23), the HXT fill (MESH-25) and fTetWild's parts (RUN-6, whose rung also goes to the speed machine when its memory holds 32 parts) use every vCPU of the machine up to 32 (MESH-25): the VM's container is given all of them as its CPU quota (`--cpus $(nproc)`), which `engines/determinism.job_cpus` reads. The plan step's rows (`engines/fea_plan_options.row`) name the same machine and its cores, and their time at that machine's cores (`fea_driver.rate_on`: the 8-thread rates times the MEASURED whole-run speed-up, 1.2 at 16 threads and 1.3 at 32 - the estimate has no per-stage split, and only the fill's share scales: 1.7x at 16, 2.2x at 32); a pass's own budget (LAD-fea-15) stays at the 8-core rate. PLAN-fea-22's quicker mesh is offered by MEMORY (`fits_standard`), never because a plan was sent to more cores. Told by cores, never the type: "Meshing on a faster machine (16 cores)"; a VM picked for memory or time "Meshing on a 16-core machine" (a vCPU is half a core); the job "Meshing on the standard machine". **The VM cannot be started** (no CPU quota - c2d has 100 vCPUs in the region -, no such machine free in any zone, no permission): the speed machine first falls to `SPEED_FALLBACK_NAME` (n2-highmem-16, the benchmark's next best: 354 s against 331), said ("The fastest machine was not available, so it meshes on another fast one"); that one, or any VM, that cannot be started either: a plan that fits the job (`cloud_fits`) runs on the job instead, started with the job's wall, and said ("The faster machine was not available...", `mesh_machine.fallback`, `adapters/mesh_execution/cloud_run_client._start_or_fall_back`); never a failed run because a bigger machine was not there. A request whose answer never came (the VM may exist) is never fallen back from; a plan that needs the VM's memory still fails as before. |
| RUN-12 | in force | **The wall's faces are priced before meshing from what the case will refine, and the size brake judges a pass when castellation ends** (`engines/snappy/wall_price.py`, `snappy_runner.render_snappy_case`, `engines/size_brake.SizeBrake`, `castellated_layer_cells`). A plan priced its wall at area / wall cell^2, but snappyHexMesh refines every feature edge two levels finer than the wall and keeps nCellsBetweenLevels cells of each level between: on an assembly with many sharp edges that band is most of the wall. The first plan now prices it: the area at the surface level plus, per side of every edge the case's own surfaceFeatureExtract angle marks, one cell of the feature level and nCellsBetweenLevels cells of each level between (RM3 as staged: 8,081 m of feature edges; priced 0.105 M wall faces before, 0.572 M now, 0.484 M built). A count learned from a stopped pass (RUN-8) still decides when there is one. And the brake reads the castellated mesh's own wall at castellation's end - the faces that take layers, times their layers, on the case's boundary files - so a pass that would be thrown away stops before it snaps instead of after (RM5's first pass: 21 of its 44 minutes castellation, 14 snapping a mesh the brake then stopped). |
| RUN-13 | in force | **A big flow mesh runs on the speed machine too, even when it fits the Cloud Run job** (rulebook 119; `contracts/mesh_machine.for_time` with the plan's `cells`, `route_cells`; Areen, 26 Nov: give CFD the same machine as FEA, cost is not a concern). A snappyHexMesh, cfMesh or multi-region snappy plan of at least `SPEED_CELLS` (1,500,000) planned cells - snappy's pass budget (its `_plan_cells`, the user's own count included), cfMesh's run-plan estimate (or the user's count when larger), the thermal plan's cells - runs on RUN-10's `SPEED_MACHINE` (c2d-standard-32, 32 vCPUs = 16 cores, 128 GB); below it, the Cloud Run job as before. Measured 26 Nov (cfdbench_lab/BENCH.md, the same part at 8, 16 and 32 CPUs, the mesh the same to 0.07% and the verdict unchanged): the whole job 1.66x / 1.83x faster on a 1.78 M external part, 1.37x / 1.55x on a 5.76 M internal part that takes three attempts, and no faster (0.96x / 0.94x) on a 5.15 M external part - snapping and layers scale, castellation balancing, the wrap, Python preparation and the checks do not; the threshold is to be tuned from site runs. **Memory still wins**: a plan whose mesher (cells x `FLOW_GIB_PER_MCELL`) needs more than the speed machine's usable memory (115 GiB, about 46 M cells) runs on RUN-9's ladder machine; between the standard machine's 8 M and that, the speed machine (more cores than the ladder's n2-highmem-8 / -16). **The same fallback ladder as RUN-10**: the speed machine, then n2-highmem-16, then - for a plan that fits it (`cloud_fits`) - the Cloud Run job, said on the live line; the machine a run really ran on is written back into the workspace (`mesh_machine.adopt_ran_on`, at `contracts/mesh_execution.run_mesh`), so RUN-5's move off the job's wall and RUN-3's step up read where it was, and a pass that fell back to the job is not sent to the VM that could not start again. **Its cores are used**: snappyHexMesh takes one MPI rank per vCPU of the container's CPU quota (at most 64, memory may lower it); cfMesh and every other OpenMP/BLAS/VTK tool take the quota too when it is above `MESH_THREADS` (8), at most `MAX_NATIVE_THREADS` (32) (`engines/determinism.machine_threads`) - so the Cloud Run job stays at 8; never the host's core count; a pinned `MESH_CFMESH_THREADS` (1 = bit-repeatable cfMesh) still wins, and the stamp records the count. **Told as FEA tells it**: every snappy pass and every builder-run mesh says its machine on the live line before it meshes ("Meshing on a faster machine (16 cores)", `contracts/narration.machine_words`), and the why record cites RUN-13 (`snappy_machine`, "faster machine") with the decision's own words ("About 4 M cells: it runs on a faster machine (16 cores) ..."). **Its parallel run must start there** (rulebook 129; `sandbox/safe_exec.usable_tmpdir`, the VM's startup script, `engines/snappy/parallel_stages.launch_failure`, `engines/snappy/drivers._stop_on_launch_failure`): the VM runs the mesh container as the image's user with TMPDIR=/work, a folder root made, and OpenMPI makes its session folder there - job c06bb745 (27 Nov, a 4.8 m car, 7.2 M planned cells, c2d-standard-32) stopped every snappyHexMesh before a rank ran ("A call to mkdir was unable to create the desired directory: /work/ompi.<host>.1000 - Permission denied"), the background box came home as the mesh and nine passes ended on "the wall came out with no faces". The VM makes /work writable for every user (1777), and a child process handed a TMPDIR it cannot write gets the folder Python proved writable instead; a run OpenMPI could not start is told as that (RUN_INFRASTRUCTURE, `launch_failed`), its background box is not sent home as a mesh, and the driver ends the attempt on the first such pass instead of re-planning it.
